<p><b>John Napier Wyndham Turner</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">CC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_Counsel" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen&#39;s Counsel">QC</a></span> (June 7, 1929&#160;&#8211;&#32;September 19, 2020) was a Canadian lawyer and politician who served as the 17th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from June to September 1984. He served as leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party of Canada</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Official Opposition</a> from 1984 to 1990.
</p><p>Turner practised law before being elected as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">member of Parliament</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1962_Canadian_federal_election" title="1962 Canadian federal election">1962 federal election</a>. He served in the cabinet of Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_and_Attorney_General_of_Canada" title="Minister of Justice and Attorney General of Canada">minister of justice and attorney general</a> from 1968 to 1972, and <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">minister of finance</a> from 1972 to 1975. As a cabinet minister, Turner came to be known as a leader of the <a href="/wiki/Blue_Grit" title="Blue Grit">Business Liberal</a> faction of the Liberal Party. Amid a global recession and the prospect of having to implement unpopular <a href="/wiki/Wage_and_price_controls" class="mw-redirect" title="Wage and price controls">wage and price controls</a>, Turner resigned from his position in 1975.
</p><p>From 1976 to 1984, Turner took a hiatus from politics, working as a corporate lawyer on <a href="/wiki/Bay_Street" title="Bay Street">Bay Street</a>. Trudeau's resignation in 1984 triggered <a href="/wiki/1984_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1984 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">a leadership election</a>, in which Turner successfully contested. Turner held the office of prime minister for just 79 days,<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> as he advised the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">governor general</a> to <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_parliament" title="Dissolution of parliament">dissolve Parliament</a> immediately after being sworn in. He went on to lose the <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984 election</a> in a landslide to <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservatives</a>, leading the Liberals to the second-worst defeat for a governing party at the federal level (in terms of proportion of seats). Turner stayed on as Liberal leader and led the Opposition for the next six years. In the <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">1988 election</a>, he vigorously campaigned against Mulroney's <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="Canada–United States Free Trade Agreement">proposed free trade agreement with the United States</a>, and led the Liberals to a modest recovery. Turner resigned as party leader in <a href="/wiki/1990_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1990 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">1990</a> and did not seek re-election in <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">1993</a>.
</p><p>Turner was Canada's first prime minister born in the United Kingdom since <a href="/wiki/Mackenzie_Bowell" title="Mackenzie Bowell">Mackenzie Bowell</a> in 1896, Canada's second shortest-serving prime minister behind <a href="/wiki/Charles_Tupper" title="Charles Tupper">Charles Tupper</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Butler_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> and Canada's fourth longest-lived prime minister, living to the age of 91.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Turner was born on June 7, 1929,<sup id="cite_ref-:2_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Martin_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Richmond,_London" title="Richmond, London">Richmond, Surrey</a>, England (now a part of London), to Leonard Hugh Turner, an English journalist with the <a href="/wiki/Manchester_Guardian" class="mw-redirect" title="Manchester Guardian">Manchester Guardian</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-nyt1984_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyt1984-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Phyllis_Ross" title="Phyllis Ross">Phyllis Gregory</a>, a Canadian economist.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He had a brother, Michael, born in 1930 (who died shortly after birth), and a sister, Brenda, born in 1931.<sup id="cite_ref-cbcarchives_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbcarchives-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> When Turner's father died in 1932, he and his sister moved to Canada with their Canadian-born mother. The family settled in her childhood home in <a href="/wiki/Rossland,_British_Columbia" title="Rossland, British Columbia">Rossland, British Columbia</a>, and later moved to <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-nyt1984_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyt1984-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner's mother was loving but demanding of her two children.<sup id="cite_ref-cbcarchives_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbcarchives-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The family was not wealthy.<sup id="cite_ref-nyt1984_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyt1984-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cbcarchives_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbcarchives-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> His mother remarried in 1945 to <a href="/wiki/Frank_Mackenzie_Ross" title="Frank Mackenzie Ross">Frank Mackenzie Ross</a>, who later served as <a href="/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_British_Columbia" title="Lieutenant Governor of British Columbia">Lieutenant Governor of British Columbia</a>, and the family relocated to <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-highlights_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-highlights-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Education"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Turner was educated at <a href="/wiki/Ashbury_College" title="Ashbury College">Ashbury College</a> and St Patrick's College, Ottawa (senior matriculation). He enrolled at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_British_Columbia" title="University of British Columbia">University of British Columbia</a> (UBC) in 1945 at age 16 where he was a member of the UBC chapter of the <a href="/wiki/Beta_Theta_Pi" title="Beta Theta Pi">Beta Theta Pi</a> fraternity, and was among Canada's outstanding track sprinters in the late 1940s.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> He held the Canadian record for the men's <a href="/wiki/100-yard_dash" title="100-yard dash">100-yard dash</a> and qualified for the <a href="/wiki/1948_London_Olympics" class="mw-redirect" title="1948 London Olympics">1948 London Olympics</a>, but a bad knee kept him from competing.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He graduated from UBC with a BA (Honours) in 1949.<sup id="cite_ref-highlights_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-highlights-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Awarded a <a href="/wiki/Rhodes_Scholarship" title="Rhodes Scholarship">Rhodes Scholarship</a>, Turner went on to <a href="/wiki/Magdalen_College,_Oxford" title="Magdalen College, Oxford">Magdalen College</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a>, where he earned a BA in <a href="/wiki/Jurisprudence" title="Jurisprudence">jurisprudence</a> in 1951, and a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Civil_Law" title="Bachelor of Civil Law">BCL</a> in 1952; his BA was promoted to an <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts_(Oxford,_Cambridge,_and_Dublin)" title="Master of Arts (Oxford, Cambridge, and Dublin)">MA per tradition</a> in 1957. He was on the track and field team at Oxford. One of his teammates was <a href="/wiki/Roger_Bannister" title="Roger Bannister">Roger Bannister</a>, who became the first runner to break the four-minute barrier in the mile.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> At Oxford, Turner was a classmate and friend of future Australian Prime Ministers <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>, as well as <a href="/wiki/Jeremy_Thorpe" title="Jeremy Thorpe">Jeremy Thorpe</a>, future leader of Britain's <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-darling_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-darling-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He also pursued doctoral studies at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">University of Paris</a> from 1952 to 1953.<sup id="cite_ref-highlights_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-highlights-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relationship_with_Princess_Margaret">Relationship with Princess Margaret</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Relationship with Princess Margaret"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On July 25, 1958, a ball was hosted by Turner's mother and stepfather (in the latter's role of Lieutenant Governor of  British Columbia), in honor of Princess Margaret at <a href="/wiki/HMCS_Discovery" title="HMCS Discovery">HMCS Discovery</a>, the Canadian navy's land station in <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>. Turner danced with <a href="/wiki/Princess_Margaret,_Countess_of_Snowdon" title="Princess Margaret, Countess of Snowdon">Princess Margaret</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> one year his junior, and they sat out talking, refusing requests to return to supper. This was the first time that Turner received significant press attention in Canada. Further meetings between them during Margaret's three week official visit to Canada led to considerable speculation about whether the two would become a serious couple. Contemporary press reports suggested there was some consternation about the reports among British officials, but their Canadian counterparts were more relaxed.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-StarDelacourt_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-StarDelacourt-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A year later, in September 1959, press attention returned when reports circulated that Turner had been a recent guest at Balmoral in August where discussions had taken place concerning marriage between the pair; that he had also paid a secret visit to see her in April and that his parents had commented on their close friendship during their visit to Scotland in August.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to contemporary press reports, <a href="/wiki/Buckingham_Palace" title="Buckingham Palace">Buckingham Palace</a> refused to engage with the rumors. A spokesperson for Margaret said the April meeting was not a secret and they had nothing to say officially about any of the reports.<sup id="cite_ref-postmarg_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-postmarg-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Turner was quoted as saying, "I have nothing whatsoever to say about these reports" <sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to letters discovered in 2015, written by Margaret to her friend Sharman Douglas and obtained by the <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Mail" title="Daily Mail">Daily Mail</a></i>, the relationship was more serious than previously thought with the princess writing in one letter, seven years later: "John Turner is here &amp; we meet on Thursday. It will seem so funny as we haven't met since I nearly married him &amp; he's bringing his wife!". Turner told the <i>Daily Mail</i>: "I have never talked about it, and I am not going to start talking about it now".<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Brenda confirmed a "very definite attraction" between her brother and the princess, but said that Turner was uninterested in royalty and would not have given up Catholicism.<sup id="cite_ref-brewster_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brewster-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It has been claimed Turner attended Margaret's party at <a href="/wiki/Balmoral_Castle" title="Balmoral Castle">Balmoral Castle</a> in August 1959 where his roommate was Margaret's future husband <a href="/wiki/Antony_Armstrong-Jones" class="mw-redirect" title="Antony Armstrong-Jones">Antony Armstrong-Jones</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-brewster_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brewster-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> however, according to his authorised biography, Armstrong-Jones visited Balmoral for the first time in early October 1959.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Turner was the only Canadian unofficial guest at <a href="/wiki/Wedding_of_Princess_Margaret_and_Antony_Armstrong-Jones" title="Wedding of Princess Margaret and Antony Armstrong-Jones">their wedding in May 1960</a>. Turner remained friends with Margaret, he and his wife often meeting the princess in Britain or during official visits to Canada. They attended Margaret's 2002 private funeral and were Canada's official representatives at the memorial service.<sup id="cite_ref-brewster_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brewster-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_and_family">Marriage and family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Marriage and family"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Turner was married on May 11, 1963, to <a href="/wiki/Geills_Turner" title="Geills Turner">Geills McCrae Kilgour</a> (b. 1937) who was then a <a href="/wiki/Systems_engineering" title="Systems engineering">systems engineer</a> with <a href="/wiki/IBM" title="IBM">IBM</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and the great niece of Canadian Army doctor <a href="/wiki/John_McCrae" title="John McCrae">John McCrae</a>, the author of what is probably the best-known First World War poem, "<a href="/wiki/In_Flanders_Fields" title="In Flanders Fields">In Flanders Fields</a>", and sister of <a href="/wiki/David_Kilgour" title="David Kilgour">David Kilgour</a>, a long-time Canadian <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">Member of Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-nyt1984_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyt1984-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The Turners have a daughter named Elizabeth and two sons: Michael and Andrew.<sup id="cite_ref-darling_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-darling-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Their second son, David, died in 2021. The Turner children attended <a href="/wiki/Rockcliffe_Park_Public_School" title="Rockcliffe Park Public School">Rockcliffe Park Public School</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> All three sons attended <a href="/wiki/Upper_Canada_College" title="Upper Canada College">Upper Canada College</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Early career"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Turner practised law, initially with the firm of <a href="/wiki/Stikeman_Elliott" title="Stikeman Elliott">Stikeman Elliott</a> in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>, Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He was elected as Member of <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">Parliament</a> for <a href="/wiki/St._Lawrence%E2%80%94St._George" title="St. Lawrence—St. George">St. Lawrence—St. George</a> in 1962 and was reelected there in every election until the riding's dissolution in 1968. He was the Member of Parliament for <a href="/wiki/Ottawa%E2%80%94Carleton_(federal_electoral_district)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ottawa—Carleton (federal electoral district)">Ottawa—Carleton</a> from 1968 to 1976.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1965, while vacationing in <a href="/wiki/Barbados" title="Barbados">Barbados</a>, Turner noticed that former prime minister and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Leader of the Opposition (Canada)">Leader of the Opposition</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a>, staying at the same hotel, was struggling in the strong <a href="/wiki/Ocean_surface_wave" class="mw-redirect" title="Ocean surface wave">surf</a> and <a href="/wiki/Undertow_(wave_action)" class="mw-redirect" title="Undertow (wave action)">undertow</a>. Turner, a competitive swimmer while in university, jumped in and pulled Diefenbaker to shore.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet_minister">Cabinet minister</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Cabinet minister"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Premiership_of_Lester_Pearson">Premiership of Lester Pearson</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Premiership of Lester Pearson"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Turner was generally respected for his work as a cabinet minister in the 1960s and 1970s, under prime ministers <a href="/wiki/Lester_Pearson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lester Pearson">Lester Pearson</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>.
</p><p>He served in the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada" title="Cabinet of Canada">Cabinet</a> of Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson" title="Lester B. Pearson">Lester Pearson</a> in various capacities, most notably as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Consumer_and_Corporate_Affairs" title="Minister of Consumer and Corporate Affairs">Minister of Consumer and Corporate Affairs</a>. When Pearson retired, Turner ran to succeed him at the <a href="/wiki/1968_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1968 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">1968 leadership convention</a>. Turner, at age 38 the youngest of the dozen leadership candidates, stated "My time is now",<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> and remarked during his speech that he was "not here for some vague, future convention in, say, <a href="/wiki/1984_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1984 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">1984</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Turner stayed on until the fourth and final ballot, finishing third behind <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> and runner-up <a href="/wiki/Robert_Winters" title="Robert Winters">Robert Winters</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Premiership_of_Pierre_Trudeau">Premiership of Pierre Trudeau</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Premiership of Pierre Trudeau"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Turner served in Trudeau's cabinet as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Justice (Canada)">minister of justice</a> for four years. Biographer Paul Litt argues that Turner was a hard-working, well-informed minister whose success was assured by his warm relationship with his peers. His achievements, say Litt, included strengthening the rights of individual defendants on trial, greater efficiency in the justice system, creation of the influential <a href="/wiki/Law_Commission_of_Canada" title="Law Commission of Canada">Law Reform Commission</a>, selecting highly professional judges, and bringing a policy perspective to the Justice Department. He led the government's position in the highly controversial <a href="/wiki/Official_Languages_Act_(Canada)" title="Official Languages Act (Canada)">Official Languages Act</a>, and he took control during the <a href="/wiki/October_Crisis" title="October Crisis">October Crisis</a> in 1970.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A leader of the <a href="/wiki/Blue_Grit" title="Blue Grit">Business Liberal</a> faction of the Liberal Party,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Turner then served as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">Minister of Finance</a> from 1972 until 1975. His challenges were severe in the face of global financial issues such as the <a href="/wiki/1973_oil_crisis" title="1973 oil crisis">1973 oil crisis</a>, the collapse of the postwar <a href="/wiki/Bretton_Woods_system" title="Bretton Woods system">Bretton Woods trading system</a>, slowing economic growth combined with soaring inflation (<a href="/wiki/Stagflation" title="Stagflation">stagflation</a>), and growing deficits.<sup id="cite_ref-Butler_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> His positions were more conservative than Trudeau's and they drew apart. In 1975 Turner surprisingly resigned from cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The Liberals had won the <a href="/wiki/1974_Canadian_federal_election" title="1974 Canadian federal election">1974 election</a> by attacking <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservatives</a> over their platform involving wage and price controls. However, Trudeau decided to implement the wage and price controls in late 1975, so some have suggested that Turner quit rather than carry out that proposal.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In a 2013 interview with <a href="/wiki/Catherine_Clark_(broadcaster)" title="Catherine Clark (broadcaster)">Catherine Clark</a> on <a href="/wiki/CPAC_(TV_channel)" title="CPAC (TV channel)">CPAC</a> Turner confirmed his resignation from cabinet was a direct result of refusing to implement wage and price controls, after campaigning against them in 1974.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his memoirs, Trudeau wrote that Turner said he resigned as Finance Minister in 1975 because he was tired of politics, after 13 years in Ottawa, and wanted to move on to a better-paying job as a lawyer in Toronto, to better support his family and to be with them more, as his children were growing up. Trudeau also suggested that Turner's years as finance minister were very difficult because of turbulent and unusual conditions in the world economy, characterized as <a href="/wiki/Stagflation" title="Stagflation">stagflation</a>, largely caused by enormous increases in the price of oil.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:left;" class=""></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Bay_Street">Bay Street</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Bay Street"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>From 1975 to 1984, Turner worked as a corporate lawyer at the <a href="/wiki/Bay_Street" title="Bay Street">Bay Street</a> law firm <a href="/wiki/McMillan_Binch_Mendelsohn_LLP" class="mw-redirect" title="McMillan Binch Mendelsohn LLP">McMillan Binch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> When Pierre Trudeau resigned as Liberal leader in 1979 following an <a href="/wiki/1979_Canadian_federal_election" title="1979 Canadian federal election">election loss</a>, Turner announced that he would not be a candidate for the Liberal leadership. Trudeau was talked into rescinding his resignation after the government of <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a> was defeated by a <a href="/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence" title="Motion of no confidence">motion of no confidence</a>, and returned to contest and win the <a href="/wiki/1980_Canadian_federal_election" title="1980 Canadian federal election">1980 federal election</a>. Trudeau then served as Prime Minister until 1984.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_minister_.28June.E2.80.93September_1984.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister_(June–September_1984)">Prime minister (June–September 1984)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Prime minister (June–September 1984)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Trudeau retired after polls showed the Liberals faced certain defeat in the next election if he remained in office. Turner then re-entered politics, and defeated <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>, his successor as finance minister, on the second ballot of the <a href="/wiki/1984_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1984 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">June 1984 Liberal leadership convention</a>. He was formally appointed prime minister on June 30.<sup id="cite_ref-darling_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-darling-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> When he was sworn in, Turner was not an MP or senator. Had he wished to have parliament summoned, he would not have been able to appear on the floor of the House of Commons. He also announced that he would not run in a by-election to get into the Commons, but would instead run in the next general election as the Liberal candidate in the <a href="/wiki/British_Columbia" title="British Columbia">British Columbia</a> riding of <a href="/wiki/Vancouver_Quadra" title="Vancouver Quadra">Vancouver Quadra</a>, a seat held by the Tories. However, this was part of Turner's strategy to rebuild the Liberals' image in <a href="/wiki/Western_Canada" title="Western Canada">western Canada</a>; at the time, the party held no seats west of <a href="/wiki/Winnipeg" title="Winnipeg">Winnipeg</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his final days of office, Trudeau recommended that <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a> <a href="/wiki/Jeanne_Sauv%C3%A9" title="Jeanne Sauvé">Jeanne Sauvé</a> appoint over 200 Liberals to <a href="/wiki/Patronage" title="Patronage">patronage</a> positions, including <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">senators</a>, judges, and executives on various governmental and <a href="/wiki/Crown_corporation" class="mw-redirect" title="Crown corporation">crown corporation</a> boards. Turner then made a further 19 appointments himself, one of Trudeau's conditions for retiring earlier than he had planned.<sup id="cite_ref-Mulroney_1991_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mulroney_1991-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1984_federal_election">1984 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: 1984 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>On July 9, only nine days after being sworn in, Turner asked Sauvé to dissolve parliament and advised her to <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">call an election</a> for early September. Progressive Conservative leader Brian Mulroney and other experts had expected Turner to tour Canada during the summer and early autumn, accompanying <a href="/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen Elizabeth II">Queen Elizabeth II</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> on their upcoming visits, and then call the election for later in the autumn.<sup id="cite_ref-Mulroney_1991_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mulroney_1991-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> As the campaign unfolded, the Tories and Mulroney, who was fighting his first general election in any capacity, soon took the lead.<sup id="cite_ref-Mulroney_1991_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mulroney_1991-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Early in the campaign, Turner appeared rusty and old-fashioned.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> His policies contrasted with Trudeau's and seemed to legitimize the Tory calls for lowering the deficit, improving relations with the United States, cutting the bureaucracy, and promoting more federal-provincial harmony. He spoke of creating "make work projects",<sup id="cite_ref-:2_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> a discarded phrase from the 1970s that had been replaced by the less patronizing "<a href="/wiki/Job_creation_program" class="mw-redirect" title="Job creation program">job creation programs</a>". Turner was also caught on television patting the bottoms of Liberal Party President <a href="/wiki/Iona_Campagnolo" title="Iona Campagnolo">Iona Campagnolo</a> and Vice-President Lise St. Martin-Tremblay, causing an uproar among <a href="/wiki/Feminists" class="mw-redirect" title="Feminists">feminists</a>, who saw such behaviour as sexist and condescending.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the televised leaders' debate, Turner attacked Mulroney over the patronage machine that the latter had allegedly set up in anticipation of victory, comparing it to the <a href="/wiki/Union_Nationale_(Quebec)" title="Union Nationale (Quebec)">Union Nationale</a> governments of <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>. Mulroney responded by pointing to the raft of patronage appointments made on the advice of Trudeau and Turner. Turner had the right to advise Sauvé to cancel Trudeau's appointments—advice that she was bound to follow by convention—but failed to do so and added to his own. Mulroney demanded that Turner apologize to the country for what he called "these horrible appointments." Turner claimed that "I had no option" except to let them stand. Mulroney responded, "<a href="/wiki/You_had_an_option,_sir" title="You had an option, sir">You had an option, sir</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;to say 'no'&#160;&#8211;&#32;and you chose to say 'yes' to the old attitudes and the old stories of the Liberal Party." He highlighted the Liberals' long record in government and resulting patronage appointments.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner discovered late in the campaign that the Liberals' electoral hopes were poor in their traditional stronghold of Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The party had heretofore relied on Trudeau's appeal, patronage, and traditional dislike of the Progressive Conservatives for victory in recent previous elections. Turner had surrounded himself with Trudeau's factional opponents and Trudeau himself did not endorse Turner.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In a last-minute turnaround, Turner rehired much of Trudeau's staff during the final weeks, but this had little effect. Quebec's disaffection with the federal Liberals regarding <a href="/wiki/Patriation" title="Patriation">patriation</a> in 1982 further contributed to their defeat. Mulroney, a native <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Quebec" title="Demographics of Quebec">Quebecker</a>, was able to harness that discontent to the Progressive Conservatives' advantage by promising a new constitutional agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On September 4, the Liberals were swept from power in a Tory landslide.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The Liberals were cut down to 40 seats, the fewest in the party's history until <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Federal_Election_2011" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Federal Election 2011">2011</a>, against 211 for the Progressive Conservatives. The Liberals fell to 17 seats in Quebec, all but four in and around <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>. Eleven members of Turner's cabinet were defeated. It was the worst defeat the Liberals experienced in a federal election since <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">1958</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner stepped down as prime minister on September 17.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> The election having been called just over a week after his being sworn in, Turner held the office of prime minister for two months and seventeen days, the second-shortest stint in Canadian history, ahead of only <a href="/wiki/Charles_Tupper" title="Charles Tupper">Sir Charles Tupper</a>, who took office after dissolution of parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Turner, along with Tupper and later <a href="/wiki/Kim_Campbell" title="Kim Campbell">Kim Campbell</a>, were the only PMs who never faced a parliament or implemented any legislative initiative.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition">Leader of the Opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ronald_Reagan_and_John_Turner_1987_crop.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Ronald_Reagan_and_John_Turner_1987_crop.jpg/220px-Ronald_Reagan_and_John_Turner_1987_crop.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="170" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Ronald_Reagan_and_John_Turner_1987_crop.jpg/330px-Ronald_Reagan_and_John_Turner_1987_crop.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Ronald_Reagan_and_John_Turner_1987_crop.jpg/440px-Ronald_Reagan_and_John_Turner_1987_crop.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1059" data-file-height="817" /></a><figcaption>Turner with President <a href="/wiki/Ronald_Reagan" title="Ronald Reagan">Ronald Reagan</a> at <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Hall" title="Rideau Hall">Rideau Hall</a>, April 1987.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1984, Turner managed to defeat the Tory incumbent in Vancouver Quadra, Bill Clarke by 3,200 votes, a surprising result given the size of the Tory wave, and became <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Leader of the Opposition (Canada)">leader of the opposition</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> He was the only Liberal MP from British Columbia, and one of only two from west of Ontario. The Liberals, amid their worst showing in party history and led by an unpopular Turner, were said by some pundits to be following the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">British Liberals</a> into oblivion. Though the Liberals had not fared much better in the <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">1958 election</a>, they had clearly emerged as the main opposition party back then. After the <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984 election</a>, however, the NDP were not far behind with 30 seats.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Their leader <a href="/wiki/Ed_Broadbent" title="Ed Broadbent">Ed Broadbent</a> consistently outpolled Turner and even Mulroney, except in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberals responded by using their large <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a> majority, built up over years of Liberal majorities in the Commons, to stall Mulroney's legislation. In addition, a group of young Liberal MPs, known as the "<a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada_Rat_Pack" title="Liberal Party of Canada Rat Pack">Rat Pack</a>", pestered Mulroney at every turn. The group included <a href="/wiki/Sheila_Copps" title="Sheila Copps">Sheila Copps</a>, <a href="/wiki/Brian_Tobin" title="Brian Tobin">Brian Tobin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Don_Boudria" title="Don Boudria">Don Boudria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/John_Nunziata" title="John Nunziata">John Nunziata</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner's leadership was frequently questioned, and in the lead up to the 1986 Liberal convention, a vote of confidence loomed large. The popular Jean Chrétien resigned his seat, creating a stir in caucus. <a href="/wiki/Keith_Davey" title="Keith Davey">Keith Davey</a> publicly voiced his concerns with Turner's leadership,<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> which coincided with backroom struggles involving Chrétien's supporters. The public conflict is said to have influenced many Liberals to support Turner, and he ended up getting a little over 75% of the delegate vote.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
The Liberals faced more internal conflict in the next few years, but polls frequently had them in front of the Progressive Conservatives (however, with Turner last in preferred prime minister categories). The upcoming <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93US_Free_Trade_Agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada–US Free Trade Agreement">Canada–US Free Trade Agreement</a> (FTA) and <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord" title="Meech Lake Accord">Meech Lake Accord</a> threatened to divide the party until Turner took the position of being pro-Meech Lake<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> and against the FTA. Turner asked the Liberal Senators to hold off on passing the legislation to implement the agreement until an election was held. It was later revealed that Mulroney planned to have an election called, anyway.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1988_federal_election">1988 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: 1988 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">1988 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>When the <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">election was called for November 21, 1988</a>, the Liberals had some early struggles, notably during one day in Montreal where 3 different costs were given for the proposed Liberal daycare program. The campaign was also hampered by a <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Canadian Broadcasting Corporation">Canadian Broadcasting Corporation</a> report that stated there was a movement in the backroom to replace Turner with Chrétien.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner campaigned rallying support against the proposed FTA, an agreement that he said would lead to the abandonment of Canada's political sovereignty to the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> His performance in the debate and his attacks on Mulroney and the FTA, where he accused the Progressive Conservative Prime Minister of selling Canada out with one signature of a pen,<sup id="cite_ref-:1_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> raised his poll numbers, and soon the Liberals were hoping for a majority. This prompted the Progressive Conservatives to stop the relatively calm campaign they had been running, and go with <a href="/wiki/Allan_Gregg" title="Allan Gregg">Allan Gregg</a>'s suggestion of "bombing the bridge" that joined anti-FTA voters and the Liberals; Turner's backbone.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The ads focused on Turner's leadership struggles, and combined with over $6&#160;million in pro-FTA ads, stopped Turner's momentum. Also not helping the Liberals was that the NDP had opposed the FTA as well (though not as vocally); this likely resulted in vote-splitting between the opposition parties. Although most Canadians voted for parties opposed to <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a>, the Tories were returned with a majority government, and implemented the deal.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-darling_18-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-darling-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberals more than doubled their representation to 83 seats, and kept their role as the <a href="/wiki/Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Official Opposition (Canada)">Official Opposition</a>; the NDP had also made gains but finished a distant third with 43 seats. The Progressive Conservatives won a reduced majority government with 169 seats.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_67-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The election loss seemed to confirm Turner's fate; he announced he was standing down from the party leadership in May 1989,<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> officially resigning in June 1990. Turner resigned as Official Opposition leader, while still holding the Liberal leadership, so <a href="/wiki/Herb_Gray" title="Herb Gray">Herb Gray</a> became the <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_leader" title="Parliamentary leader">caucus leader</a> in the interim.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien won that year's <a href="/wiki/1990_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1990 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">leadership convention</a> over <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin" title="Paul Martin">Paul Martin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Although not officially endorsed by Turner himself, Martin was widely the favourite of Turner's supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner continued to represent Vancouver Quadra in the House of Commons before retiring from politics in the 1993 election.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="After_politics">After politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: After politics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Turner_(39436619124)_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/John_Turner_%2839436619124%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-John_Turner_%2839436619124%29_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="308" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/John_Turner_%2839436619124%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-John_Turner_%2839436619124%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/John_Turner_%2839436619124%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-John_Turner_%2839436619124%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1735" data-file-height="2430" /></a><figcaption>Turner in 2018</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1990, Turner returned to practicing law, this time working for Miller Thomson LLP; this was despite him serving as an MP for another three years. He eventually became head of the firm’s scholarships program. Turner was also a board member for several corporations.<sup id="cite_ref-TurnerCanEncyclopedia_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TurnerCanEncyclopedia-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner was a member of the Canadian delegation that monitored the runoff vote in the <a href="/wiki/2004_Ukrainian_presidential_election" title="2004 Ukrainian presidential election">2004 Ukrainian presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-TurnerCanEncyclopedia_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TurnerCanEncyclopedia-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turner voiced his support for the <a href="/wiki/Campaign_for_the_Establishment_of_a_United_Nations_Parliamentary_Assembly" title="Campaign for the Establishment of a United Nations Parliamentary Assembly">Campaign for the Establishment of a United Nations Parliamentary Assembly</a>, an organisation which campaigns for democratic reformation of the United Nations, and the creation of a more accountable international political system.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2017, he was awarded the Gold Medal of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Geographical_Society" title="Royal Canadian Geographical Society">Royal Canadian Geographical Society</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Turner lived in the <a href="/wiki/Deer_Park,_Toronto" title="Deer Park, Toronto">Deer Park</a> neighbourhood of Toronto.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_state_funeral">Death and state funeral</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Death and state funeral"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_John_Turner_gravesite_photo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Prime_Minister_John_Turner_gravesite_photo.jpg/220px-Prime_Minister_John_Turner_gravesite_photo.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="332" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Prime_Minister_John_Turner_gravesite_photo.jpg/330px-Prime_Minister_John_Turner_gravesite_photo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Prime_Minister_John_Turner_gravesite_photo.jpg/440px-Prime_Minister_John_Turner_gravesite_photo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1341" data-file-height="2026" /></a><figcaption>Turner's grave site in Mount Pleasant Cemetery</figcaption></figure>
<p>Turner died on September 19, 2020, at the age of 91.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:3_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> A <a href="/wiki/State_funerals_in_Canada" title="State funerals in Canada">state funeral</a> was held for Turner on October 6, 2020, at <a href="/wiki/St._Michael%27s_Cathedral_Basilica_(Toronto)" title="St. Michael&#39;s Cathedral Basilica (Toronto)">St. Michael's Cathedral Basilica</a>. The ceremony was scaled back due to the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic" title="COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</a> with masking and <a href="/wiki/Social_distancing" title="Social distancing">social distancing</a> protocols in place and attendance to be limited to 160 guests with no <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_state" title="Lying in state">lying in state</a> being possible.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Turner was buried in a private service at <a href="/wiki/Mount_Pleasant_Cemetery,_Toronto" title="Mount Pleasant Cemetery, Toronto">Mount Pleasant Cemetery</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Turner and Mackenzie King are two former Prime Ministers interred at Mount Pleasant.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Turner&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Honours"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="center">

<td>
<ul><li>2012</li>
<li>As a former <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">Prime Minister of Canada</a> a Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a> and a member of the <a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">Queen's Privy Council for Canada</a> the Right Honourable John Turner would be awarded the medal as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_order_of_precedence" title="Canadian order of precedence">Canadian order of precedence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
</td></tr>
</tbody></table>
<p>According to Canadian protocol, as a former prime minister, he was styled <i><a href="/wiki/The_Right_Honourable" title="The Right Honourable">The Right Honourable</a></i> for life.
</p><p>Turner was ranked 18th out of the first 20 Prime Ministers of Canada (through <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>) by a survey of Canadian historians in 1999. The survey was used in the book <i>Prime Ministers: Ranking Canada's Leaders</i> by <a href="/wiki/J._L._Granatstein" title="J. L. Granatstein">J. L. Granatstein</a> and <a href="/wiki/Norman_Hillmer" title="Norman Hillmer">Norman Hillmer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Turner was appointed a Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a> on October 19, 1994, and was invested on May 3, 1995. His citation reads: </p><blockquote><p>He became Canada's seventeenth Prime Minister, crowning a distinguished parliamentary career during which he held several key Cabinet portfolios. Parallel to his political life, he has been a respected member of the law profession and supporter of many charitable organizations, in particular Mount Sinai Hospital and the Community Foundation of Toronto. His passion for his country is admired by all Canadians.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<table class="wikitable mw-collapsible" style="max-width: 100%">
<caption class="nowrap">Coat of arms of John Turner
</caption>
<tbody><tr>
<td style="width: 220px;"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Turner_Escutcheon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Turner_Escutcheon.png/220px-Turner_Escutcheon.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="261" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Turner_Escutcheon.png/330px-Turner_Escutcheon.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Turner_Escutcheon.png/440px-Turner_Escutcheon.png 2x" data-file-width="664" data-file-height="789" /></a></span>
</td>
<td style="min-width:175px;">
<dl><dt>Crest</dt>
<dd>Issuant from a coronet érablé Gules the rim bearing a frieze of alternating dogwood lily and trillium flowers all Argent a demi wolf Or charged on each shoulder with a poppy Gules seeded Sable and bearing in the dexter forepaw an ansul Gules;</dd>
<dt>Escutcheon</dt>
<dd>Gules four canoe paddles their handles conjoined in cross between four canoes Or on a canton the mark of the Prime Ministership of Canada (Argent four maple leaves conjoined in cross at the stem Gules);</dd>
<dt>Supporters</dt>
<dd>On a grassy mound rising above barry wavy Argent and Azure set between two stems growing thereon roses Gules thistles and shamrocks Or and standing in front of river cedar trees Vert dexter a caribou buck Or gorged with a collar of river cedar branches Vert pendant therefrom the badge of a member of the House of Commons of Canada sinister a caribou doe Or gorged with a like collar pendant therefrom a medallion per bend barry wavy Azure and Argent and Vert;</dd>
<dt>Motto</dt>
<dd>Esse Quam Videri (To Be Rather Than To Seem) <sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
</td></tr></tbody></table>
